CHARACTER: Korvash Emberhide, Dragonborn barbarian 2, acolyte background (as the sheet is now, after the last round; the level and HP may have been lower earlier)
== OPENING ==
Your character is complete.

The mountains stand over the lowlands like a wall, and everything beneath them lives with an eye turned upward. Herders drive their flocks down from the high pastures to the market towns, and some come down with stories of the warbands that raid the passes. The Iron Tallies are the worst of those stories: hobgoblins who count their raids in notches.

Korvash Emberhide is a red-scaled, broad-shouldered dragonborn, loud and proud, and he is the son of a clan of mountain herders. When he was a boy, the Iron Tallies overran his village. His father Dargan held the Goat's Throat pass, losing a hand and then his life, so the herd and the children could flee. His sister Tessa was lost in the raid, and no one ever learned her fate. Korvash was raised in Harrowmere by his Aunt Veshra. He carries his father's second-best greataxe, with Dargan's ember-red scale-sash tied to the haft, and he has spent years making sure no one weak is bullied while he stands nearby.

Dusk settles over Harrowmere's market square. Lanterns are being lit and stallholders are folding awnings. Three young toughs scatter into the alleys, one of them clutching a wrist and swearing, and they leave behind an overturned cart of cheeses and a family of herders huddled against it. The mother is gathering the wheels back into her apron, and the father is nursing a split lip.

A thin boy of about twelve, mud to the knees, breaks away from them and runs up to you, breathless. "You're Korvash, aren't you? Veshra's nephew? She sent me to find you, but she's not the only one who wants you." He swallows. "Drovers came down from the high road this morning. They saw a hobgoblin band, with a captain in a tin mask carrying a rod covered in notches. They're camped below the Goat's Throat, and they're taking children from the herding steadings. Please... my little sister's up there with our uncle's flock."

The family watches you, and the market has gone quiet. What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 1 ==
PLAYER: I crouch down to the boy's level, set a hand on his shoulder, and say, "What's your name, lad? Your sister's name too. I'll bring her home, and that's my word." Then I stand, help the herder family right their cart so they're not left in the dust, and tell the father to get his wife and kids to Aunt Veshra's door while I gear up and head for the Goat's Throat tonight.

Korvash leaves for the pass tonight. The route leads to the pass where his father died and to the question of Tessa."}
  [game] notebook: created an entry for Bring Wren Home
  [game] time: 20 minutes later: Day 1, 6:50 PM
DM: The boy's chin trembles, but he stands straighter under your hand. "Pell, sir. Pell Harrow-side. My sister's Wren. She's seven, she has a red scarf, and she hums when she's scared." He wipes his nose on his sleeve. "She'll hum. You'll hear her."

"Then I'll listen for it," you tell him.

You set your shoulder to the cheese cart and heave it upright, and the father scrambles to stack the wheels back on. The mother catches your scaled hand in both of hers and says nothing, which says enough. When you tell them to go to Veshra's door and say Korvash sent them, the father nods hard, wipes his lip, and herds his children off through the lanterns. Pell hesitates, then runs after them, glancing back twice.

The market is still quiet as you leave. A stallholder presses a heel of bread and a wedge of hard cheese into your hands without a word. Aunt Veshra's house is only two streets away, and the road north is the other way. The mountains are going black against a purple sky, and the Goat's Throat lies a night's hard march up that road.

You still have 15 gp, ten days of rations, and ten torches. It is about seven in the evening. Do you stop at Veshra's door first, to say a word and take her counsel, or do you head straight for the high road?

== ROUND 2 ==
PLAYER: I stop at Veshra's door first, because she sent Pell for me and she's earned a word. I'll keep it short: tell her I'm going up to the Goat's Throat after Wren, ask what she knows about the Iron Tallies' camp, and see if she'll hold onto Pell's family until I'm back.

  [game] time: 15 minutes later: Day 1, 7:05 PM
DM: Veshra's door opens before you knock. She is a lean, grey-scaled dragonborn with flour on her forearms and your father's jaw. She looks at your axe, then at your face, and sighs like someone who has long expected this.

"The boy found you, then." She hears you out without interrupting, and when you ask about the camp she pulls you into the kitchen light. "Hale Orrin, the drover, came through at noon. They're at Cinder Ford, where the road crosses the stream under the Throat. Forty tents, he said, maybe fewer, with fires and a tin mask walking among them. Two scouts sit the old herd track above the ford. Take the north goat-path around them, the one your father used to bring the lambs down. You'll remember it."

You do remember it. She doesn't say the rest, but her eyes go to the sash on your haft.

She presses a pouch of dried goat meat into your hand and, with it, a small horn charm on a thong, worn smooth. "Dargan carved it. Wear it. Come back and wear it at my table." Then, quieter: "Pell's family will sleep here, and no one will touch them while I'm breathing. Go and do what you've sworn. And Korvash, if you find the cairn, you may stop there. You don't have to be loud with him."

She shuts the door softly. Beyond the last lanterns of Harrowmere, the high road climbs into the dark, and the first stars are out above the peaks.

You have the horn charm and the goat meat now. The road is a night's march. Do you push on straight through, or make camp partway and arrive fresh at dawn?

== ROUND 3 ==
PLAYER: I tie the horn charm around my neck, tuck the goat meat in my pack, and tell her, "I'll wear it at your table, Aunt." Then I push on through the night, taking the north goat-path my father used so I can slip past the two scouts above Cinder Ford before dawn, and I'll rest in the rocks nearby if I need it, because Wren can't wait for a morning.

  [game] notebook: updated Cinder Ford
DM: You march through the dark with the horn charm knocking against your chest. The road climbs, and then the stars show you the line of the old goat-path, a pale scratch on the slope. But the dark was kinder to your memory than to your feet. Somewhere past a split boulder the path forks and you pick the wrong fork, and for two long hours you are working along scree and thorn, finding the path again only by the cold smell of the stream.

It is a little after two in the morning when you come out on a rocky shoulder above the Goat's Throat. Below you, the ford is a cluster of low red fires and dark tents, and a single tall figure walks between them, the firelight catching a flat pale gleam where a face should be. Smoke rolls along the water.

To your right, perhaps sixty yards off along the ridge, a small coal glows in the rocks, with two shapes beside it: the scouts. They are above the line you meant to take, and they haven't seen you yet. The path you lost would have run well below them. From here, a cairn of stacked stones stands out against the sky farther up the pass.

Faint on the wind, from the camp, comes a thin sound: someone humming.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 4 ==
PLAYER: I go flat to the rock and stay there, jaw tight, listening to that humming. Wren's alive, and that's all I needed to hear. I'll take the scouts quietly before they raise a shout: creep along the ridge in the shadows, close to within handaxe range, and cut them down fast with the axe if they spot me. Dargan's cairn can wait until she's safe.

DM: You go belly-down along the ridge, keeping the rocks between you and the coal. Your breath runs slow and your scales stay dark against the stone. The humming drifts up from the camp now and then, and each time it does your jaw tightens, but you keep your pace. The scouts never turn their heads. The one nearest the coal has his helmet off, picking at a bone. The other sits with a longbow across his knees, watching the road below, and his back is to you.

You settle in behind a boulder about fifteen feet from them, close enough to hear them muttering in Goblin about the cold and how long until relief. Both are hobgoblins in iron-banded mail and shields, and a tin-striped tally is painted on each breastplate. They don't know you are here.

The camp is far below, and the nearest tents are a long run downhill. A shout from either of these two would carry. You have the dark, the boulder, and the first move.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 5 ==
PLAYER: I wait for the archer to look back down the road, then I rage and charge out from behind the boulder, going for the archer first with the greataxe so he can't raise a shout or loose an arrow. If the one with the bone gets up, I'll drop him next before he can yell.

The archer's sword was a clumsy lash that did not find you, and your rage turned aside the edge.

Your ruling-level problem is plain: you have perhaps three or four rounds before the camp's runners reach this ridge, and the tin-masked captain will be among them. Both hobgoblins are still on their feet, and the archer is bleeding at your boots.

DM: You surface slowly, as if from deep water, and the first thing you know is cold stone under your cheek and a taste of iron in your mouth.

You remember falling: the archer's blade, the picket's, then the weight of the dark. You remember the picket's voice over you, curt and unhurried in Goblin. "Not the throat, fool. The captain wants this one breathing. Tally him." Hands were on your chest after that, hard but practiced, binding something tight. After that there was nothing.

It is nearly dawn, about a quarter past five. You are propped against the wheel of a supply cart at the edge of the Iron Tallies' camp, wrists bound behind you with leather cord and ankles hobbled. You have 1 hit point and a body that feels like one big bruise. Your greataxe, your handaxes, and your javelins are gone. The horn charm still hangs at your chest, so they didn't think it worth taking. Grey light is spreading across the ford, and the smoke of the cookfires lies low over the water.

Forty feet away, where a stake line has been driven into the mud, a huddle of small shapes sits on the ground, bound at the wrist and tied to the line: a dozen children, maybe more. One of them, a small girl in a red scarf, is humming very quietly, and she hasn't stopped.

Two hobgoblins stand guard over the line with bows. Across the camp a tall figure in a tin mask is looking straight at you, a long rod notched along its whole length in his hand. He says something to the picket beside him and begins to walk toward you.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 8 ==
PLAYER: I stay slumped and let him come, but I lift my head and meet that tin mask dead on, spitting blood into the mud. "You're Gorrak Vael. My father took your hand at the Goat's Throat, and I'm Dargan's son. Whatever you want me breathing for, say it, but those children go free first."

Behind my back I'm quietly working the cord against the cart's iron wheel rim, feeling for a rough edge, and I keep my eyes flicking to Wren's red scarf so she knows someone's heard her humming.

  [game] notebook: updated Gorrak Vael
  [game] time: 2 minutes later: Day 2, 5:17:24 AM
DM: Behind your back the cord rasps against the rim. It frays and bites into your wrists, but it won't part, and you keep at it as quietly as you can.

The tin-masked hobgoblin stops an arm's length off. Up close the mask is crude, hammered from a cook's pot and riveted at the temples, and behind its eye-slits his gaze is slow and level. The rod in his hand has more notches than you can count. Two hobgoblins with drawn blades stand a pace behind him.

"Dargan's son," he says in Common, flat and dry, without a trace of heat. "I know the sash. I know the name, and I know your father lost the hand. I took nothing from him but a morning and a pass." He tilts his head at the children. "Your voice carried well. Good. A voice is worth something."

He crouches until the mask is level with your eyes. "Look at them. I have thirty-one on the line and the road to the tin-mines is long, and children sell cheap in bulk. But I am short of axes. You killed none of mine, which is a mercy to both of us, though you were two seconds from it. Swear on your word, the word your clan is known for, to carry an axe for the Iron Tallies for one year and a day. Then the line is cut at Harrowmere's border and the children walk home. I keep my word too, boy. Ask anyone who has met me."

Behind him, Wren's humming has faltered. The little girl in the red scarf is looking at you with her whole face.

The cord is frayed but still holds, and you have 1 hit point and no weapon. What do you say?

== ROUND 9 ==
PLAYER: I keep my eyes on his mask and keep sawing behind my back. "You say you keep your word, so hear mine: I'll swear, but the children are cut loose and walking before I do, and I want Wren and the rest within sight of Harrowmere's border with my own eyes. Do that, and Dargan's son will carry your axe for a year and a day."

  [game] notebook: updated Gorrak Vael
DM: Gorrak listens to all of it without moving, and then something that could be a laugh comes dry through the tin.

"A bargain-maker. Dargan was the same." He shakes his head slowly. "No. A sworn word buys the line, and a conditional word buys nothing. You swear first. Then the line moves out at noon with my sergeants and with you walking at its head, unbound. At the border stones they are cut loose. You will see it, as you ask, and you will see it with a blade at your back." He straightens. "I give you the time it takes the sun to clear the ridge. Think quickly, boy. The tin-mines are patient but I am not."

His shadow lifts off you. Behind your back the leather finally gives with a soft tug and parts, though you keep your wrists together and say nothing. Two hobgoblins with drawn blades stand within reach, three more at the stake line have bows, and the camp is waking all around you. The cart wheel is at your back, and the children are forty feet off. You are at 1 hit point with no weapon in your hands, and your gear is somewhere in this camp.

Gorrak waits for your answer. What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 10 ==
PLAYER: I let the silence sit a moment, then lift my chin. "Then I swear it, Gorrak Vael, on my father's blood: a year and a day, my axe for the Iron Tallies, and every child walks free at the border stones. I keep my word, so you'd better keep yours."

I keep my wrists tucked behind me with the cord hanging loose, and add, "And my father's axe comes back to my hand, or what use am I to you?"

  [DM write_lore (hidden)] {"entry": "Gorrak Vael", "add_to_description": "Day 2: accepted Korvash's oath. Wants him at the head of the line. Axe returns at the border stones. Has told Korvash to rest first; camp breaks around noon."}
  [game] notebook: updated Gorrak Vael
DM: You say it clearly enough for the whole ford to hear: on your father's blood, a year and a day, and every child to walk free at the border stones. For a long breath nobody moves. Then Gorrak Vael lays two fingers of his free hand against the rod, as if marking something no one else can see.

"Sworn." He turns the tin mask to the picket. "Cut him loose. Water, bread, and a place to sit. Let him not be dead of a cough before the border."

"His father's axe," he adds, without looking back, "comes home when the stones do. Not before. You have had one hour's use of a bargain already, boy. Don't spend it on cleverness."

The picket cuts the frayed leather and does not remark on how little there was left to cut. They give you water, a heel of black bread, and a flat stone by a dying fire. You sit. Whether it is the bread or sheer stubbornness, the shaking leaves your hands. Across the camp, someone has taken pity on the children and a hobgoblin cook is carrying a pot of porridge along the stake line. Wren eats with both hands and watches you over the rim of the bowl, and when she sees you looking she holds up one grubby fist, thumb tucked in, in the clan sign for "I'm well."

By mid-morning the sun has cleared the ridge and the camp is breaking. Tents come down in practiced silence, mule teams are being hitched, and the line of children is being roped together for the road. You have your wits again, and about eight hit points. You are unarmed among forty-odd hobgoblins, sworn to a year and a day, and the road south toward Harrowmere's border stones is a long one.

Above the pass, the cairn stands against the sky. The line is not yet moving. What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 11 ==
PLAYER: I stand slowly and walk to Gorrak, hands open. "Before we march, give me the time it takes to walk to my father's cairn and back, with a guard at my shoulder if you like. I've kept my word to you, and I'll keep it, but I owe Dargan a word too."

If he grants it, I'll touch the horn charm and say my piece quietly, the way Aunt Veshra said I could, then come back and take my place at the head of the line.

  [game] time: 25 minutes later: Day 2, 11:13:24 AM
DM: Gorrak Vael studies you through the mask for the length of two breaths. "Sergeant Ushk goes with you," he says at last. "Half an hour. A man who leaves his father unspoken to gets sloppy." He turns away, already done with it.

Ushk is a broad, scar-faced hobgoblin who walks a pace behind your left shoulder with his sword sheathed and his hand never far from it. He says nothing the whole climb. The old goat-path is steeper than you remembered, switchbacking up through the thorn and the loose scree, and every turn of it is a memory you'd rather not have: the place where you fell as a boy and your father picked you up one-handed; the ledge where the lambs were counted.

The Goat's Throat is narrow, as it always was: a notch of grey rock barely wide enough for two men abreast, with the wind funneling through it like breath. The cairn stands just inside the mouth, taller than you recall, a mound of fitted stones with a flat slab at its head. Someone has cut Dargan's name into the slab in Draconic, the letters weathered soft. Lichen has crept over the older stones, but the top of the cairn is clean.

Among the stones at the base, tucked in a gap where the wind can't take it, sits a small goat carved from horn, no longer than your thumb, with a ribbon of undyed wool tied about its neck. The ribbon is not grey with weather. It has been here a season, perhaps, but not years.

Ushk stops three paces back and folds his arms. The wind is the only sound.

What does Korvash say, and what does he do?

== ROUND 12 ==
PLAYER: I kneel at the slab, lay my hand flat on the cold stone, and let the wind carry off the loudness for once. "Father, I'm sorry it took me this long. I swore a hard oath today to buy thirty-one children their lives, and I'll keep it the way you taught me, but I won't break faith with you either."

Then I pick up the little horn goat and turn it over, because someone has been here recently and left it for Dargan, and I want to see if it's carved like his work or someone else's. And I ask quietly, without turning round, "Ushk, did your warband leave anything here, or did someone else come to this cairn?"

DM: You kneel with your palm flat on the cold slab and say it low enough that only the stone and the wind can hear: the apology, the oath, and the promise that you'll keep faith with both of them. The wind goes on moving through the Throat, and for a moment the loudness really does leave you. Behind you, Ushk shifts his weight and says nothing.

Then you pick up the little horn goat. It is a child's work: lopsided legs, one horn longer than the other, the cuts shallow and eager. Turning it over, you find a mark scratched into the belly, a small crescent with a single notch bitten out of its curve. You know that mark. It was never your father's. It is the mark Tessa cut into every spindle, bowl, and peg she ever owned when she was small, her own invention, and she scratched it into your boots once, the winter she was six. No one else in the clan used a notched crescent. You are sure of that.

The ribbon knotted at the goat's neck is fresh wool, not yet gone grey.

You ask Ushk the question without turning. He is silent long enough that you think he has decided not to answer. Then, in the clipped Common of a soldier giving a report: "Tallies leave nothing at graves. Two moons back at dusk, a patrol saw one by the stones. Small. Herder's cloak, hood up. It saw the patrol first and ran into the high rocks, quick as a goat. The patrol wanted to hunt it down." He pauses. "Captain said no. Said leave it be. I have not wondered why."

The wind has moved the ribbon. Ushk clears his throat, because the half hour is nearly spent. The line is waiting below, thirty-one children, and the border stones are a long day's walk.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 13 ==
PLAYER: My throat is tight, and for once I'm not loud. I close the goat in my fist, then set it back exactly where she left it. With the tip of my knife-edge of a thumbnail and a flat stone, I scratch her notched crescent into the base of the cairn, and under it in Draconic: "Tessa. Korvash lives. Harrowmere, Aunt Veshra's door."

I stand, wipe my face with the back of my hand, and say to Ushk, "That's what I owed him. Lead on, Sergeant, and mark where the captain said to leave her be, because I will be asking him why."

DM: Gorrak does not answer at once. The tin mask stays on you long enough that you hear a mule stamp somewhere behind the column, and the rod shifts in his hand, a thumb moving over one notch, then another.

"A rod holds a tally of what is taken," he says, very quietly. "Not every mark on it is that. Some are what is owed." He turns his face away toward the road. "You will have the answer at the stones, boy, with your axe in your hand. Not on a road with sergeants listening. I have said more than I meant to."

You do not like it, but it is an answer and it has the sound of truth. You turn, give Wren the nod and the thumb tucked in, and she answers with her own fist and falls in behind you with a small, stern face. You set the pace the smallest legs can keep.

It is a slow day's walk. The road drops out of the high pastures by switchbacks and stone steps, with the mule trains clanking behind and hobgoblin sergeants walking the margins. They do not strike the children, which surprises you, and they hand water along the line at each halt. At one rest a boy no older than six asks you in a whisper if you are really going to take them home. You say yes, and keep saying it, in different words, until he sleeps standing against your arm.

By evening, the light is going copper on the western slopes when the column halts at an abandoned drovers' fold, a long stone enclosure with a half roof and a spring. Fires are lit. The children are bedded down in the lee of the wall under hobgoblin blankets. Gorrak's tent goes up at the far end, and Ushk stations himself on a rock where he can see you. By a sergeant's count, the border stones lie perhaps ten miles on, a morning's walk.

You have the evening to yourself, as much as a sworn man under guard has anything, and the children are close. What does Korvash do?

DM: You stop at the tent flap where Ushk and the nearest pickets can see you, hands open, and say it low and level through the canvas. There is a pause. Then Gorrak's voice, dry as ever: "Come in, then. Leave the shouting outside."

The tent is bare. A bedroll, a map-case, a lantern, a chair he does not sit in. He stands with the rod across his palms and has taken off the tin mask. Behind it is a hobgoblin's face, older than you pictured, grey at the temples and seamed with a burn scar that runs from jaw to ear. He sets the mask on the bedroll as if it were a heavy thing.

"No, I do not know that it is your sister," he says. "You will want me to say yes or no, and I can't. At the Goat's Throat your father and I bargained, with his hand in the dirt. The children for the axe. He refused. I counted the ones who ran that night, as I count everything. One of the clan was never accounted for. A girl. I notched the rod for her, and then I struck it out." He turns the rod so you can see: one notch, scored through. "A hooded figure has watched my band two moons, no more. Quick as a goat. It does not run from me. It runs from the others."

Something falls into place for you as he speaks, the way a stranger's face sometimes does. You are watching his hands, and they are not steady on the rod. When he glances at the tent flap, it is not at Ushk. He is afraid, and not of you. You catch the name he doesn't say, in the shape of his jaw as he swallows it.

"The tin-mines, boy." His voice drops. "Greyshaft. There is a Foreman there who counts hands the way I count raids, and every season I owe him a quota. Thirty-one children are not a quota. They are *my* quota. If I cut them loose at the stones, I come to him short, and short counts get a rod laid across a back. So I take an axe. A year and a day of yours covers the shortfall, if he accepts it." He meets your eyes. "That is what you swore into. I will still cut them loose. I gave my word. But now you know what it costs, and I would like to see how much you can carry."

He lifts the mask and sets it back over his face, and it changes him back into the thing from the ford. "Go and sleep, Dargan's son. The stones are a morning's walk."

It is a little before eight. You have been told a great deal, and you still have your word, a long night, and a sleeping line of children. What does Korvash do?

I turn at the flap and add, "And if that hooded girl comes to you before she comes to me, you tell her Korvash lives." Then I go back to the sleeping children, take the wall at my back, and try to sleep with one ear open.

DM: Your forehead meets his nose with a crack you feel in your own teeth, and your arm locks about his sword-arm. Then your weight does the rest. One by one the hobgoblin blades whisper home. On the far side, Veshra's crook comes down slowly, and the two hunters let their strings go slack.

Gorrak Vael has not moved from the rope. He is looking at you through the tin mask, and for a moment you cannot read it at all. Then he walks to a pack mule, unbuckles a bundle, and carries it across the stones himself. Your greataxe is in it, the red sash still knotted at the haft, along with your handaxes and javelins. He holds the haft out across both palms.

"Your father's axe," he says. "As sworn."

You take it. It is heavier than you remember, and the sash is warm in the sun. Behind him the children are streaming toward the villagers. Wren breaks free and runs, red scarf flying, and does not stop until she has thrown her arms around your leg and pressed her face into it. A moment later, a boy of about twelve is running up the road from Harrowmere, mud to the knees, shouting her name.

Gorrak watches that too. When he speaks again, it is quiet. "You will find me on the road at the Throat in the dark of the moon, and I will tell you where the Foreman wants his axe. A year and a day, Korvash Emberhide. Don't be late, and don't make me come looking." He turns and goes back up the line to his column.

You've earned 100 XP for the bout with Brakk. It is half past eleven. Wren is at your leg, Pell is almost to you, and your aunt is walking up the road with her chin up and tears on her face. What does Korvash do?

He was goat-keeper to your father's herd and he's the only one left who walked those rocks with Dargan."

You do, that evening. Hobb Teyl is eighty if he is a day, cataracts in both eyes, but he lights up at your father's name. He tells you about the Weeping Ledge, a shelter above the Throat hidden behind a water seep, a crack in the cliff only Dargan's own household ever knew. "He showed his children. Only his. If a child of that house lived and wanted to hide, it'd be there."

The days after are quiet, and mostly yours. You mend your pack, sharpen the greataxe, and drink Veshra's soup. You carry Pell's family's water. In the market you ask quietly and often. On the fourth day the peddler Marl Quill, a salt-seller with a tongue like a rasp, tells you over a mug of small beer that a hooded girl has twice left bundles of goat cheese on the back step of the Seven Crooks inn and taken nothing for them but a few copper and, once, a carved horn token in change. Dusk, always. Not seen for a week.

It is the evening of the tenth day. You are rested, hale, with every ember of your rage banked and ready. The dark of the moon is tonight or tomorrow, and the high road north is a day and a half away at a forced pace, or two at an ordinary one. Veshra is at the door with a pack she has put together without being asked.

What does Korvash do?

You take the high road at a hard pace, with the axe on your shoulder and the sash knotted tight. The light goes out of the sky over the first hour, and then the moon, which is absent tonight, doesn't come up at all. It is the darkest road you have ever walked: stars and the pale scratch of the track and your own breath, no more. The weight of the pack, the clank of the handaxes, and the steady climb are a kind of comfort.

You make the foothills near midnight, and you go looking for the goat-path to the Weeping Ledge by Hobb Teyl's directions. But you cannot find the turning in the dark. Twice you think you have it, and twice it dwindles to scree and thorn. By the time you give it up, you have come out into the notch of the Goat's Throat itself, with the wind funneling over you, and the cairn is ahead.

It is a little after midnight. Someone has been here. Beneath the words you scratched, fresh pale lines in the stone catch the starlight: the notched crescent, and under it a single Draconic word cut hard and quick. *Not yet.*

The horn goat is gone from its gap.

And beside the cairn, with his back to the wind and his rod resting across his arm, stands a tall figure in a tin mask, who has plainly been waiting for you.

"You are early, Emberhide," says Gorrak Vael. "Or I am late. Come and stand where I can see you."

What does Korvash do?

DM: You keep the axe low and your voice lower, out of respect for the stones. Gorrak watches you come, and when you stop he does not take the tin mask off this time, though his hand rises toward it and falls.

"I saw the marks as you did," he says. "I came up the east path an hour after dusk and found them already cut, the goat gone. She is quicker than my scouts and kinder to the rocks than they are." He lets that sit. "Counting, you ask. Both, boy. I will not insult you."

He turns the rod over in his hands. "Your father held this notch with one hand and a goat-herd's axe. I had a score of my own with me and could have gone through him in half a morning, and he made me work for it all the same. While we were at it, a small thing went by on the high ledge, quick, in the dark, behind the cairn-stones he'd already stacked. I saw it. I did not raise my hand. I have asked myself since what I owed him that morning that I paid in that coin." The mask turns to the cuts in the stone. "The Foreman's scouts hunt that ledge too. I have walked those rocks for two seasons to keep them off, and that is what 'leave it be' meant."

He straightens. "As to the axe. Greyshaft's smelter galleries need a hard man who does not cough at fire. A dragonborn's fire is useful to a Foreman. He asked for one by name, and the name he asked for was yours, because I told him what I had seen at the ford." Something almost like shame crosses the voice, flat though it is. "I will carry that. You report at Greyshaft's gate by midwinter's eve, three days' road from here. I would have you leave at dawn."

The wind has changed. You are at the cairn of your father, with a hundred stars and no moon, a hobgoblin who held the pass against him and let your sister live, and a word freshly cut in the stone that says *not yet*. The Weeping Ledge is somewhere in the dark above you.

DM: Gorrak says nothing as you go, and does not take a step to follow. You hear the rasp of tin on the edge of his hand as he turns the rod over, and after a time his footfalls recede, west along the notch, toward his own fires.

You take the east path with the sash knotted on the haft and your hands empty of torchlight, because Dargan never carried one in these rocks and the stars are enough. You climb by feel, and as you climb you call: your father's call, three notes rising, low and steady in Draconic. *Tessa. It's Kor. I'm not leaving without you.* The wind takes the words up and throws them against the stone, and you listen for an answer with everything you have, and you hear only the wind and, now and again, a loose pebble skittering from a ledge that may have been dislodged by a goat or a girl or your own heel. You can't tell which.

You find three seeps in the dark. None is the Weeping Ledge. Dawn comes up grey and cold over the eastern ridges and finds you on a shoulder of rock with your voice gone to gravel, the cold in your bones again, and nothing in your hands.

Then, as the light creeps down the path, you see it. On a flat stone at the next bend, set square in the middle of the way where you could not miss it, sits the little horn goat. Its ribbon is new, a fresh strip of wool the color of dried blood. Under its feet is a chip of black slate, and scratched into the slate, in a hand you last saw at six years old, grown quick and angular but still hers, is a line of Draconic.

*Greyshaft. Third gate. Don't let him see you know me.*

You turn the chip over. On the back is the notched crescent, cut deeper than the rest, like a signature.

It is a little after half past five on the morning of day eleven. Gorrak's fires are a smudge of smoke to the west. You have until midwinter's eve to reach Greyshaft, and now you know who will be waiting at its third gate.

What does Korvash do?

Let the Foreman see exactly that.

By six the cairn is behind you, and the road to Greyshaft runs north and east over the high ground, three days of cold rock and thin pasture. Midwinter's eve is coming, and at its end waits a gate, and a girl who has not yet decided to be found.

DM: Out of game for a moment, two things. You have 15 gp, unchanged, and that is all the coin you carry. As for midwinter's eve, I hadn't pinned its date when Gorrak named it, so here is my ruling: it falls on day 20, nine days from this morning. The road to Greyshaft is three days long. You have slack, and Gorrak's "leave at dawn" was a captain's impatience more than a deadline. I've noted it in the record.

Back on the road. You walk the high ground at a hard, steady pace, and you do not just walk. You read the land the way your father taught you: the lines of the ridges, the places a man would stand if he wanted to see a road without being seen on it. You keep your fire small at night and your axe sharpened, and you practice the face you will wear at the gate until it fits without effort.

On the second afternoon, near three o'clock, you find them. It is not the movement that gives them away but the lack of it. Half a mile off on a bare ridge to the east, two grey shapes sit where goats and ravens should be, and one of them is too still to be a rock. Two hobgoblins in grey cloaks, bows slung, keeping pace along the ridge with you. They have been there, you think, since this morning. They do not close the distance, and they do not hide the fact that they are watching. They are meant to be seen. It is a message and a leash.

The road runs on ahead, and the afternoon light is long and cold. You have a day and a half of road left and almost eight days of slack, with the Foreman's eyes already on your back.

What does Korvash do?

That night I'll make a big, honest fire for them to see and sit with my back to the light, sharpening the axe slowly and counting out who's watching, so that by Greyshaft they'll think they know me.

  [game] notebook: updated Foreman's Scouts
  [game] time: 1 day 15 hours later: Day 14, 5:58:36 AM
DM: You give the ridge your two-finger salute, slow and sour, and walk on grumbling in Goblin about wolf-leashes and tin-mine wages loud enough to carry across the empty ground. That night you build a big, honest fire, sit with your back to the flames, and sharpen the greataxe slowly while you count who is watching.

It is a good performance, and a little too much of one. You were never a subtle man, and your best acting is the same as your loudest self, so the sullen sneer comes out with the bellow still in it. You see it too late. The nearer of the grey cloaks has sat very still all evening on his ridge, and when you glance up he is not watching the fire. He is watching you the way a man watches a trick he has almost seen through. At dawn one of them is gone. The other keeps pace with you the rest of the way, a half-mile off, patient as a stone.

The road climbs through a long grey day and a longer grey night. On the morning of day fourteen, a little before six, you crest a rise, and there is Greyshaft.

It sits in a bowl of black scree: a stockade of iron-banded timber, three gates one above the next up the slope, and behind them the dark mouths of the galleries, each with its smear of smoke. Smelter chimneys breathe over the whole valley, and the air tastes of cold metal. Hobgoblin sentries walk the wall in steady pairs. Below the lowest gate, a line of carts and shackled laborers waits to be let in.

The lowest gate is the one you must report to. Somewhere above it, the third gate stands against the grey sky. You have six days to spare before midwinter's eve, and a grey cloak is watching your back.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 30 ==
PLAYER: I won't dither on a hill with a grey cloak at my back. I walk straight down to the lowest gate, join the line of carts and chained laborers, and when I reach the sentry I say it flat and short, no sneer this time: "Korvash Emberhide, son of Dargan. Sworn to the Iron Tallies a year and a day, and sent for by your Foreman. I'm early, so take me to him."

I keep my mouth shut after that, and my eyes on the gates climbing the slope, counting guards and counting steps to the third.

He is heavy and fastidious, in a leather apron with a ring of brass tallying beads at his belt and a ledger open on a standing desk. He does not raise his eyes when you enter.

"Emberhide." His voice is soft, almost kind. "Dargan's son. The Iron Tallies' hired axe." He finishes his line in the ledger, blots it, and only then looks up. His eyes are flat and clever, and they run over you from boots to horns exactly as a butcher's would, weighing without malice. "You walk well for a man who has been bought. Better than my scouts reported. They tell me you play at sullen, and that you do it poorly."

He closes the ledger. "Three days ago you stood at your father's cairn at the Goat's Throat, an hour before dawn. My scouts did not see why. I would like to hear it from you."

Two guards stand either side of the door. A scribe sits behind a screen, pen poised. The Foreman waits, his thumb on the page.

What does Korvash say?

I tilt my chin at the ledger. "Count that however you like. I swore an oath on my father's blood and I keep it, so put me to work, and tell your scouts to find something better to chew on than a son's grief."

DM: You say it the way you say everything: loudly, with your chin up and every word meant. The scribe's pen scratches behind its screen. The guards by the door shift their feet. When you finish, the Foreman looks at you for a long moment, and his thumb moves once across the edge of the page.

"You will guard the door of the second smelter gallery. The heat is considerable. You will be fed, and you will sleep in the lower barracks. A bunkmate will show you the ways of the place."

A small goblin boy appears at the screen as if summoned: thin, bright-eyed, ink to the elbows, a clerk's cap sliding over one ear. "Tally-Pip, sir!" he chirps, and gives you a grin that is both friendly and perfectly attentive. You do not doubt that he writes down everything you say.

"One more thing," the Foreman adds as you turn. "The third gate is closed to hired axes. It is where the ledger sleeps. Do not climb to it. Do not look up at it." The softness has gone out of his voice and left only the level weight. "I would be most displeased."

The rest of the morning goes to marching and a bowl of grey porridge, to the smell of coal and iron, to the second gallery's door, a slab of riveted plate with a gap through which heat rolls out like breath. It is nearly one in the afternoon. Tally-Pip chatters at your elbow the whole way, naming the guards, the cooks, and the shifts, and from the corner of your eye you watch him count how many questions you do not ask.

The third gate is above and behind you, forbidden, and somewhere up there is a girl who left you a slate chip. You have six days.

The lower barracks are a long low hall that smells of wet wool, coal and boiled barley. You take the bunk against the wall, tuck your pouch beneath your head with the goat and the slate inside it, and eat your porridge slowly while the room talks around you. You learn who gambles and who sulks, who owes whom, which sergeant has a cough. Tally-Pip drops onto the next bunk with his ledger and falls asleep mid-sentence, cap over his eyes.

The next day passes at the gallery door in heat and noise: a shift bell, a water cart, a hobgoblin with a hammer. You keep your eyes on the dirt. At the noon bell the limping boy brings water and a heel of bread. You think about not thinking.

At dusk, when the bell has tolled and the second-wall guards have begun, quietly, to pass a flask, you hear it. At first it is only a thread under the forge noise and the wind: a woman's voice, or a girl's, thin and unhurried, floating down from the gate-top where the washing hangs. You know the tune before you know the words. It is the clan's goat-counting song, the one Dargan sang at the evening fold when you were small and could not sleep, a nonsense chant of numbers and animals that ends in one goat missing and coming home.

She sings it quietly, in Draconic, all the way through. Then, where the seventh goat is lost and found again, she goes back and sings the verse a second time, slower and plainer, so that anyone who knew it would hear.

None of the guards pay it any mind. Pip, beside you, doesn't look up from his page, except to say, "Mender's singing again. She does that. It's creepy." He scratches something out.

It is just after six in the evening of day fifteen, and the song has stopped. The wash-line is still. You have not looked up. Five days remain, and you have a hundred ways to get a message to a girl you are not allowed to know.

What does Korvash do?

  [game] notebook: created an entry for Break the Foreman's Ledger
  [game] time: 25 minutes later: Day 16, 10:53:36 PM
DM: You pull your boots on, wade onto the flat rocks, and stop at the mouth of the arch with your hands open and the handaxe still in your belt. The water is loud and cold around your ankles. Low and steady, above the trickle, you hum the first three notes of Dargan's evening call, and then you say it in Draconic: "Tess. It's Kor. Nobody followed me. Come out where I can see you."

For a long moment nothing answers. Then, from the dark of the arch, very quietly, a voice finishes the next three notes of the call, a little flat, as though it has been a long time since anyone sang them to anyone.

She steps out into the starlight with the hood pushed back. She is smaller than you remember anything being: lean and wiry, hardly more than a breath taller than your shoulder. Her scales have gone the rust-red of old iron, dulled by years of soot and cold. One horn is broken off short, and a burn scar runs up her left forearm in a ladder of pale seams. Her eyes are your father's eyes, yellow-amber and level, and they look at you as though she is memorizing something she is afraid will vanish.

"Kor," she says. Then: "You got *fat*."

You laugh. It comes out cracked and wrong, and you have the axe out of your head and your arms around her before you've decided to move. She does not hug back at first, stiff as a post. Then her fingers dig into the back of your tunic and hold on hard enough to hurt, and her shoulders start to shake without any sound at all.

It is a minute, or ten. When she steps back she scrubs her face on her sleeve and the look in her eyes has hardened into purpose. "No time. Listen." She speaks in short clipped sentences, the way you learn to when every word costs. "I hid on the ledge the night they came. Dargan's stones covered me. Gorrak's man saw me go, and let me." Her mouth twists. "I lived on goat-farms. Cheese. Whatever. A year ago I heard the tin-masks sold a hundred children a season to a mine. So I came to see. I got on as a mender. I've been up in the ledger-house since autumn." She pulls a fold of grey cloth from her cloak, and in it a tiny scrap of black paper with writing on it, columns of names. "The Foreman writes every hand he owns. Every child. Every date. He moves the ledger and a hundred of them to the deep shaft on midwinter's eve, four days from now. After that, they're gone. Nobody can find them. Nobody can count them."

She looks up at you, and her chin is Dargan's chin. "I can't do it alone, Kor. I couldn't even climb to the cairn. But I can get you up the stair. Will you help me burn his book?"

It is nearly eleven at night. The wall is quiet above you, with Ruk's flask somewhere up there, and the sluice runs cold over your boots. You have kept your word to the Tallies for eleven days of a year and a day, and the girl you went looking for is here, alive, asking you for something that would break it.

What does Korvash say?

== ROUND 38 ==
PLAYER: I hold her by both shoulders and say, "Dargan's oath came first, Tess: stand between the weak and whoever means them harm, and that's a hundred children going down a shaft. I swore Gorrak my axe, not my silence, so I'll burn his book, and then I'll stand before the captain with the ash on my hands and answer for it."

I check the dark arch behind her and drop my voice. "Tell me the stair, how many Tally-sworn guard it, and where the ledger sleeps, because I'm in."

Her eyes go bright, and she looks away to hide it. For a moment she looks six years old. Then she nods once, hard, the way Dargan used to nod at the end of a counting, and the girl is gone again and the mender stands in the water in her place.

"Then listen." She crouches on a flat stone and draws in the wet with one finger, fast and sure. "Third gate. The ledger-house sits on top of it, one stair. Another stroke. "The ledger sleeps in an iron-bound chest in his bedchamber. The key is on a chain at his belt, and he sleeps like a hawk. There's a second book, a copy, in the Counting House by the lower gate. If we burn one and not the other, we've done nothing, so both."

"The nights of the eighteenth or the nineteenth," she says. "Before the move. Any later and they're in the deep shaft, and I don't know the way."

Behind her the water drips and the arch is silent. Up on the wall someone laughs and a flask clinks, and Ruk's voice calls a name, then falls quiet. It is a little after eleven, and you must be in your bunk before the dawn bell.

You have three nights to prepare and a sister who has just asked you to commit your oath-bound axe to arson. What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 39 ==
PLAYER: I crouch beside her and look at the drawing in the wet. "Then it's the night of the eighteenth, and we do it in order: the copy in the Counting House first, because it's nearest my gallery and I can pull it down off the shelf with my own hands, and then I climb your drain-stair for the ledger itself. Where are the children kept, Tess, and how do we get them out once the books are ash?"

I touch the broken stump of her horn gently. "Until then you stay hooded and you stay dull, and you don't come to the culvert again. I'll hang a red thread on my belt at dusk the day before if the way is clear, and I'll keep Pip fed and talkative so he writes down boredom instead of me."

DM: You crouch beside her in the wet and look at the drawing she has scratched there, and then you take the stick out of her hand and mend it, because the lines for the Counting House are wrong and you have stood at its door. "The copy first," you say. "It's by the lower gate, nearest my gallery, and I can pull it off its shelf with my own hands. Then I climb your drain-stair for the ledger itself. Where are the children kept, Tess, and how do we get them out once the books are ash?"

She answers without looking up. "The Tin Row. A barred bunkhouse cut into the cliff under the first gate. Ninety-four of them. A warden called Hask and four guards. The door key's on the Foreman's chain, but the Row has an old ore-chute at its back. I found it on a washing day. It runs out to the scree on the east side." She sketches it, then rubs the drawing out with her palm. "It's tight. Small ones first."

You touch the broken stump of her horn, very lightly. "Until then you stay hooded and stay dull, and you don't come to the culvert again. At dusk the day before, I'll hang a red thread on my belt if the way is clear. And I'll keep Pip fed and talkative, so he writes down boredom instead of me."

She almost smiles. Then she is gone up the sluice-path without a sound, a rust-colored shadow, and you stand alone in the cold water for a minute until you can trust your legs.

You make your bunk well before the dawn bell, with no one the wiser and Pip snoring under his cap. Two days go by as days do in a mine: heat and bells and the dull ache of standing. You feed Pip strips of goat meat and let him chatter. He writes *bored, hums, sharpens axe*, and you read it upside down over his shoulder with a straight face.

It is just before six in the evening on the eighteenth, and the dusk bell is about to ring. You have a red thread in your pocket, a handaxe in your belt, and a greataxe leaning against the gallery door. The second wall's flask is already going round. Above you at the gate-top, a line of washing hangs: grey, grey, grey.

Tonight is the night. What does Korvash do?

DM: Out of game for a moment, to answer your question: in Dargan's fold signals, grey cloths alone meant fair weather, with nothing to report and nothing changed, so you hold your plan and keep to the day's work. Three greys at dusk is Tessa telling you that the stair is as she described it, no new guards and no new trouble. Nothing has gone wrong above.

The dusk bell rings out over the valley, long and low. You tie the red thread to your belt in plain sight, where anyone looking down from the gate-top could see a stray scrap of color, and then you plant your feet at the gallery door again and keep your eyes on the dirt.

Pip is at your elbow with his ledger, chewing a strip of goat meat. "You've got a bit of string on your belt," he says, around the meat. You grunt that it's a family thing, and he writes *family string* and loses interest.

Ten minutes later, the wash-line at the gate-top changes. The two grey cloths come down and a single red one goes up in the middle of the line, alone, snapping once in the cold wind. You do not look at it. You know the signal. *Go now. The way is open.*

The shift bell for the second-wall guards rings, and the flask begins its round. Ruk's mate is already laughing at something. It is just after six in the evening, with the second bell about four hours off. The Counting House sits by the lower gate, a squat building of stacked slate with a barred window and a lamp burning in it: a night scribe, two door guards, and the copy of the ledger on a shelf behind them.

Your plan was the copy first, then the stair. What does Korvash do?

  [game] notebook: updated Warden Hask
DM: You come back to the world slowly, and the first thing you know is cold iron around your ankle and the smell of sweat, wet straw, and damp stone.

You remember the door going in, the lamp in your fist, the oil catching, and the ledger flaring up like a torch. You remember the guard in the doorway, and the dark. You do not remember anyone dragging you. A dull weight on your chest feels like a hand that has not yet let go.

You are lying on straw against a rock wall with a manacle on your left ankle, chained to a ring set in the stone. Your belt is gone, and the pouch with it, and so are the greataxe on your back and the handaxes and the javelins. Someone has bound your side and thigh with strips of coarse cloth, tight and not unkindly. It is a little after eight at night, and you are at 1 hit point.

The place is a long low chamber cut into the cliff, with a barred door at one end and a single lamp burning on a hook. Along both walls lie rows of small shapes in straw, wrapped in blankets: ninety-odd children, most of them awake, all of them silent. Dozens of eyes are on you. This is the Tin Row. A girl no older than ten, a few feet away, is watching you with the slow stare of someone who has learned not to be surprised. At the barred door a hobgoblin sits on a stool with a ring of keys in his lap, methodical and joyless, not looking at you at all. Warden Hask.

Your pouch, with the carved goat and the slate chip and your last coin, is gone with the rest. You think of Tessa on the roof of the third gate, listening to a bell that has stopped. You think of a ledger in a chest, intact, and a hundred children moving to a deep shaft in two nights' time.

At the back of the long chamber, in the shadow behind the last row of bunks, a draft is moving the lamp-flame. Somewhere back there is the old ore-chute.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 46 ==
PLAYER: I keep still and low, eyes half-lidded so Hask thinks I'm out, and turn my head slowly toward the girl who's watching me. In a whisper no louder than breath, in Common, I ask, "What's your name, little one? Do you know the chute at the back, and who of you can fit it?"

While she thinks, I run my fingers along the manacle and the ring in the wall, feeling for a rusted pin or a loose bolt, because I'm not walking out of here without every one of these children.

  [game] time: 15 minutes later: Day 18, 8:23:36 PM
DM: You keep still and low, eyes half-lidded, the way a man lies when he's taking stock of his own ribs, and you turn your head a fraction toward the girl. Your whisper is no more than breath in the straw. "What's your name, little one? Do you know the chute at the back, and who of you can fit it?"

She doesn't startle. She looks at Hask, at his stool and his bent head, and then at you, and answers in a voice smaller than yours. "Ninna." Her eyes are old for ten. "The grate's fifteen paces behind the last bunk. A square one, bolted from the outside. Only the little ones fit. Not me, maybe. Not the big boys." She hesitates. "The mender came two nights ago. She brought bread. She said to wait until a goat sings. We don't know what that means."

Hask turns a page in whatever he is reading, and does not look up. The lamp flame wavers in the draft from the back. Your fingers have been busy under the blanket they covered you with, running along the iron cuff on your ankle and the ring where its chain meets the rock. It is solid work. The cuff is riveted, the ring is sunk deep, and there is no pin to work and no bolt to wiggle. Whoever made it did not make it carelessly.

Ninna sees your face and nods very slightly, like someone who has had the same thought. She tips her chin at the warden. "He keeps all the keys in his lap. That one's for you. He counts us at dawn and dusk."

It is just after half past eight. You have one hit point, a cuff you can't pick, a warden with the only key in his lap, and a sister somewhere above who has heard the alarm and does not know whether you are alive. In two nights the Row is emptied and the master ledger goes with it.

What does Korvash do?

Then you lie slack and grey with your breath shallow, and in the dark, as if it were nothing, you sleep. It is the sleep of a man who has been taught by the mountains to take what rest he is given. No one comes in the night. The lamp gutters low. Somewhere the wind moves along the cliff. When you wake, your wounds have knit and the stiffness has gone out of your joints. You are whole again, with the rage banked in your chest and a fire in your lungs, but you are careful to keep your face the face of a man at death's door.

Pre-dawn comes with a scrape of iron. At the dawn bell, a quarter before six, Hask stands, stretches his back, and makes his count. He walks the rows with the lamp, tapping children's shoulders with the tip of a rod. Around you a ragged chorus of coughing starts, then low moans, then a small voice: "The big one's gone cold, Warden. He won't wake." Another: "He's breathing funny."

Hask stops in front of you and looks down. His face is blank and lined and without malice, exactly the face of a man counting grain. He sets the lamp on the floor, crouches, and extends a thick hand toward your throat to feel for the pulse. The ring of keys hangs at his belt, within arm's reach. Behind him, the stool, the barred door, and four guards are asleep in a clump at the far end.

You are chained at one ankle, but not at the hands. His fingers are inches from your neck. It is just before six in the morning. What does Korvash do?

One by one, the smallest children are lowered through the chute into waiting hands, and you can hear, outside, a woman's hoarse voice counting them off by name.

A sentry is leaning on the parapet, pointing down the slope to the east scree. "Oi! Hask! There's brats on the rocks! Whose bloody count is this?"

His voice carries. He turns his head, and he is looking directly at you, a figure in a helm and cloak at the door of the Tin Row, standing too straight and too tall, with horns pushing at the iron. A second sentry beside him lowers a spear, and the first begins to take the stairs down toward you at a trot.

DM: You squeeze through the torn frame on your belly with the shield scraping rock above you, and the cold dawn hits your face. There's no oil flask on your belt, only your torches and tinderbox. Whatever Greyshaft took off you in the night, it took that too, so the door stays as it is and the wedge will have to hold on its own.

Tessa is there, flat against the scree with her hood thrown back, and she has your arm before you've fully cleared the opening, hauling you upright with a strength that doesn't match her size. She looks at your side, at the black blood on the bandage, at your face, and her mouth goes thin. "You are an idiot," she says in Draconic, and doesn't let go.

The east slope is a wasteland of grey scree and broken slate. Strung out across it, crouched behind boulders in bunches of ten the way you told them, are the children. Ninna is counting heads with her lips moving, one hand on the shoulder of each as she passes. She reaches the end, looks at Tessa, and nods once. Ninety-four. Nobody has been left behind.

Behind and above, the alarm bell has begun to ring out over Greyshaft, long and angry, and from the Row's door you can hear the sentries bellowing for axes to break it. You have a quarter hour at most, perhaps less. East is the stone gully Tessa scouted, a goat track dropping toward a stream and the high road beyond. Harrowmere is three days' march for children on foot.

Tessa keeps her voice low. "I can lead them down the track. I can't walk them all the way and hold off hobgoblins too. And the book is still up there. He'll move it tomorrow, with whatever hands he has left."

At the first tired boy, you hoist him onto your shoulder without a word, shield on one arm, sword hand free.

At the narrowest place in the gully the walls close to a spear's length, loose slate stacked in dark leaning sheets overhead. You set your back to it, find the keystone slab and heave. Your arms are not what they were an hour ago. The slab shudders and a trickle of shale slides across the path, and then it stops. The slope shrugs, and half of what you wanted comes down: a ragged heap you could climb in a minute, no wall.

You are still staring at it when Ninna's whisper cuts back down the line. "Two of them. On the ridge."

Two grey-cloaked figures stand against the lightening sky on the lip of the gully, thirty feet above the track and forty behind the last of the column. They are the Foreman's scouts, the ones who watched you on the road. One has a short bow already nocked. The other has a longsword and is looking at the children, not at you. Neither has shouted. They are waiting to see how many of you there are.

It is a little before seven in the morning. You are at 2 hit points with a boy on your shoulder, a sword and a shield, the narrow gully at your back, and the whole column strung out ahead of you in the open. What does Korvash do?

It carries. The archer takes a half step back and his string slackens. The swordsman looks at the length of the gully, at the tiny figure on the rubble that has not so much as flinched, and at what the camp's alarm bell must have sounded like from here, and he does not move.

"We're not paid to die on slate," he calls down at last, flat and sour, in Goblin. "Foreman's orders were follow and count. Not fight." He spits over the edge. "Rider's gone to Cinder Ford already, for the Tally captain. Vael will be on this road by dusk, and he can fetch you himself."

They do not come down. They do not go away either. They melt back from the lip of the ridge, and for the next half hour you catch a grey shape or two on the skyline, always the same distance behind, always keeping count.

It is a little after seven. The column is a mile down the track now, and the children have been walking in silence since the bell. Through the dark hours you work your wrists against the iron whenever the line bunches, turning them, feeling for a give. The cuffs are well made. They do not give. Worse, you are not as sly as you thought. Around midnight a heavy hand closes on your elbow, and Sergeant Dreg's thick-necked bulk looms beside you, smelling of oil and old garlic. He says nothing. He takes a short cord from his belt, ties your chain to the belt of the spearman ahead of you, and pulls the knot hard enough to make you stagger. "Walk, axe," he says, in Goblin. "You'll pull no tricks on my road." Your wrists are raw beneath the iron, and you will be more careful.

You march on, hour after hour, through the cold, a short halt for water, then more climbing. Now it is a little before four in the morning on midwinter's eve, and the sky has gone to the colour of slate. At a switchback, where the road doubles back round a spur of black rock, your eye catches something at the verge. A strip of grey cloth is tied to a thorn bush, stirring in the wind. Below it, on a flat stone, there is a crescent scratched in the dust, fresh, with a single notch beside it. It is Tessa's mark, and the notch is the one she used for *I am near.*

Nobody else has seen it. Dreg is three men ahead, and the spearman on your cord is looking at his feet. Gorrak walks at the head of the line, steady as ever, rod in hand. The road ahead climbs toward the shoulder of the mountain, and behind it, somewhere past the dark, Greyshaft waits. You are still at 5 hit points and still in iron, with a day's march left.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 67 ==
PLAYER: I let my heel drag as I pass the stone, scuffing a short line in the dust under her crescent in Draconic: *Dusk. Lower gate. Ledger moves. Gorrak's number burns with it.* Then I stumble on purpose, grunt a curse at the spearman on my cord, and kick the dust across it so only she could read it.

After that I keep marching with my eyes down and my jaw set, saving what strength I have for dusk.

DM: Your heel drags as you pass the stone. A short line in the dust under her crescent, in Draconic, scratched with the side of your boot: *Dusk. Lower gate. Ledger moves. Gorrak's number burns with it.* Then you stumble, grunt a curse at the spearman on your cord, and scrub the dust flat over every mark. Dreg turns his head at the noise and sees only a prisoner who cannot walk straight. He growls and turns back.

After that there is nothing but the road. You march through the grey dawn and the long white day, over the shoulder of the mountain and down through a country of black rock and thin snow. There are three short halts for water, and nobody speaks. The cords chafe your wrists to the meat. You save your strength the way a goat saves it, in small mean amounts, and the ache in your side stays a throb. You are still at 5 hit points.

Late in the afternoon you come over a final ridge and see it laid out below: the black scree bowl, the smelters smoking, the three gates stepping up the slope, the Counting House by the lowest one with its roof burned open to the sky. It is a quarter to six on midwinter's eve. The light is going the colour of a bruise, as it was the first night you stood in that bowl.

Gorrak halts the column on the lip of the ridge. Below, on the lower gate's wall, torches are being lit. Down in the yard a long line of figures is shuffling toward the dark mouth of the deep shaft. It is the Tin Row's leavings, the hands that remain, with hobgoblins in grey cloaks on either side and, near the third gate, a heavy figure in a leather apron with a string of brass beads in his hand, watching the line go down. Behind him, two Tally-sworn carry an iron-bound chest on a litter.

You see no sign of Tessa. The dusk bell is about to ring.

Gorrak turns the mask toward you. "Now," he says quietly. "Tell me again what I should remember."

What does Korvash say or do?

== ROUND 68 ==
PLAYER: I look at the chest on the litter, then at the mask. "Remember that chest holds your number, Captain, and the key hangs on the Foreman's belt, no more than three paces from where you'll stand to present me."

"Bring me before him, strike the cuffs off in front of his clerks as if to show a broken dog, and when I move for that chain, you either cut it or you cut me. Either way, you'll know which man you are."

DM: The mask does not move for a long moment, and the wind pushes grit along the ridge between you. You say it all: the chest, the key, the three paces, the clerks watching a broken dog. When you stop, you have the strange feeling of having handed a man a knife by the blade.

"You ask a great deal of a man who has said he will do nothing," Gorrak says. His voice is flat, but the rod in his hand has gone still. "Walk. Keep your hands low. I promise you nothing, Emberhide, and I will not say which man I am until I have seen it."

Then the column goes down the scree in the failing light, and the dusk bell begins to toll.

The lower gate opens for the Tallies, and the yard receives you, a trampled court of cinders and slate under flaring torches. The deep shaft's mouth gapes at the far end, a black square in the rock, with the last of the Tin Row's leavings shuffling toward it between grey cloaks. They are mostly older children, grey-faced, ankle-chained in a row, thirty or forty of them. Nobody is crying. The Foreman is exactly as you remember him: fastidious, soft-handed, a string of brass beads drifting through his fingers. At his belt a chain hangs, and on the chain, a heavy key. He does not smile. He looks at your wrists, then at your face, and the beads stop. "Captain Vael," he says, in his small, gentle voice. "How good of you to bring my property back." His eyes slide past you, to the column, to the road behind it. "I count one hand where I was promised ninety-four."

Six Tally-sworn stand on the stair behind him, spears upright. The chest is four paces from you, the key at his belt perhaps three, and Gorrak has stopped a step to your left with his hand resting on his sword hilt. High on the third gate, at the wash-line, a single red cloth has just gone up, snapping once in the wind.

You are at 5 hit points, unarmed and in iron, and the dusk bell is still tolling. What does Korvash do?

DM: You fill your lungs and the fire goes out of you in a low red cone across the Foreman, the two hobgoblins hauling the chest, and the litter's dry wood. The Foreman twists aside with a hiss and a scorched sleeve. The two bearers take it full in the face, and the litter flares. The chest's iron bands blacken, but the ledger inside it is another matter. You roar to Tessa to burn it down.

The reply is the flat edge of a longsword. The first guard misses you and the second does not. The blow comes down on the hit point you had left, and the world goes out like a lamp.

You come back in the dark, shackled by both wrists and ankles to a ring in a wall of cold wet rock, with your head ringing and a dull weight on your chest. The air smells of lamp-smoke, tin and fear. It is a little after seven in the evening, and you are at 1 hit point. The Foreman's guards have taken your cloak and helm, and the horn charm is gone from your neck. Someone, you suspect, took it as a keepsake.

You are in the deep shaft. It is a wide low gallery cut into the mountain, lit by two sputtering torches, with a heavy door at one end. Along the wall in the dim, shackled in a long row of their own, sit the last of the Tin Row's hands, thirty or forty children, silent, watching you. At the far end of the gallery a litter rests on the floor with a scorched chest on it, its iron bands blackened and its lid shut. Two Tally-sworn stand beside it, and the one nearest has a patch of singed beard.

Gorrak Vael is not here. You do not know if he is alive. Of Tessa there is no sign, no song, and no cloth. The only sound is a slow drip, and far above, very faint, the dusk bell, ringing again.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 71 ==
PLAYER: I keep still and low, eyes half-lidded on the two guards at the chest, and whisper to the nearest child down the row, "I'm Korvash, I walked ninety-four of your friends out of the Tin Row, and I won't leave you here. Did you see where they took the big captain in the tin mask, or a hooded girl with a broken horn?"

Then, very quietly under the drip of water, I begin humming the goat-counting song, so that if Tessa is anywhere in these rocks she'll know where to find us.

  [game] notebook: updated Tessa
DM: The whisper passes unnoticed. The two guards at the chest are arguing in low Goblin about whose turn it is to hold the torch, and the drip covers the rest. The boy beside you, ten at most, with a split lip and a front tooth missing, stares at you a long moment before he answers in a voice like a cricket's.

"I know you. You're the big one from the gallery door." He swallows. "They dragged the mask-man up the third-gate stair, two grey cloaks with him. He wasn't moving. The Foreman went up first, holding his side, and he was *cross*." He glances at the guards, lowers his voice further. "I didn't see a girl with a broken horn. But there was a girl on the wall with a red cloth, before they shut the door. She was fast. I saw her go over the parapet, and nobody shot her."

You begin to hum. The goat-counting song, low and rough, the first verse and the second, under the drip. You are on the sixth goat when it comes back to you through the rock: three soft taps, very faint, then a pause, then three more. The rhythm is the counting song's, the seventh goat's, and it comes from the far wall of the gallery, behind the row of children, where an old black seam of rock runs from floor to roof. Nothing else moves. The guards don't turn.

It is half past seven. Above you, the slow bells ring the hours of midwinter's eve, and the chest sits on its litter, its iron bands scorched, thirty feet away with two guards beside it. You are at 1 hit point, shackled at wrist and ankle to a ring in the wall, with the key nowhere in reach.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 72 ==
PLAYER: I answer on the iron itself, tapping the ring with my shackle chain to the last two lines of the song, timed to fall in the gaps between the drips so the guards hear only water. Then I whisper to the boy, "Pass it down the row: whatever happens, nobody cries out, and when the wall opens, the littlest go first."

I keep my eyes on the two guards and the chest, counting their breaths and their torch changes, ready for the moment Tessa makes her move.

DM: You tap the ring with your chain, the last two lines of the song, one tap to the gap between drips, so that the guards hear only water. Down the row the whisper goes, nudge by nudge, the way it goes in any herd: nobody cries out, and when the wall opens the littlest go first.

You get your answer sooner than you hoped. At the far end, where the black seam of rock meets the floor, a crack of darker dark widens by the width of a hand, and a small shape slides out of it on its belly, its hood pulled low, and flattens itself among the children. Tessa. She crawls along the base of the wall to you, never standing, and her fingers find the lock on your left wrist and set to work, a hair-thin pick clicking in the dark.

It does not go well. The first lock fights her, and the pick skates off the inside of the cuff with a scrape like a fingernail on slate. It's loud, quite loud, in the stillness of the shaft. Tessa freezes with her breath in her teeth.

At the far end one of the guards looks up, the one with the singed beard. He says something short in Goblin to his partner, picks the torch off its hook, and starts down the row between the children, slowly, with his hand on his sword hilt. Eighteen paces off. Twelve.

The torchlight swings across the wall and the children, and across you, one hit point and two cuffs, with Tessa's hood hunched against your ribs. The ring bolts you to the wall. The first lock is half-picked, and the boy beside you has gone absolutely still.

What does Korvash do?

== ROUND 73 ==

My half-picked hand stays hidden under me, and if he bends down to check on me, I grab his sword arm and drag him in against the wall.

DM: You never see the blow that ends it. You go under in the dark of the shaft with Tessa's voice in your ear, and the last thing you know is her knife, small and bright, and a guard's face cut open with rage. The next thing you know is a candle.

You come back slowly, on a hard floor, to the smell of beeswax and lamp-oil and old paper. It is a little before ten at night, and you are at 1 hit point. Your wrists are shackled again to an iron ring let into a heavy table-leg, and your ankles are hobbled. You are in a long low room under a beamed roof, shuttered against the wind, and a stove is ticking in the corner. The master ledger lies open on a desk under a lamp. Beside it sits the iron-bound chest from the litter, its lid blackened but whole.

Tessa lies on the floor a pace from you with her hood thrown back, breathing, her wrists bound in front of her and her left forearm bandaged over the old scar. She is not awake. A few feet beyond her Gorrak Vael lies on his side on a pallet, trussed with cord, his mask gone. His face is the one you have never seen but once: greying, jaw to ear a pale seam of burn. His eyes are open and are fixed on you.

Behind the desk the Foreman writes. His side is bandaged tight under the apron, his soft hands are steady, and the brass beads click at the end of each line. He does not look up until he has finished the page. "Emberhide," he says mildly. "Two lines tonight. Hand: one. Mender: one. The captain I have not yet decided on." He blots the page and turns the ledger a little so that you can see it. Your name is there, in a neat clerk's hand, with the date, and beside it a number. Beneath it, Tessa's.

"You burned my copy and you hurt my side, and I should be angry with you," he says, in the same patient tone. "But I find I'm not. A hand that fights is worth three that don't. I will ask you one question, and I would like you to answer it truthfully, because it concerns your sister, and she is not strong." He lays down the pen and folds his hands over the beads. "Where is the rest of the ninety-four?"

Six Tally-sworn stand along the walls, spears upright, and one of them has a singed beard. The door at the far end is barred. The wind mutters at the shutters. Gorrak has not moved a muscle, but his bound hands have found each other behind his back.

You are at 1 hit point, with no rage and no breath to spend. Nothing in the room is yours. What does Korvash say or do?

Then, lower: "Take what you want from me, the axe, the year, my neck, but touch Tessa and I'll find you in whatever dark you crawl into, even with my hands chained. Cut her cords and let her sleep, and then we'll talk."

DM: You give him the loudest, hoarsest laugh you have, and it rattles off the beams. You tell him he can count the ninety-four himself. You tell him you'd bite out your own tongue before naming a road, and that if he lays a finger on Tessa, you will find him in whatever dark he crawls into with your hands chained. You mean every word.

It lands wrong. You hear it land wrong. The laugh cracks halfway, the threat comes out of a throat gone raw, and what the room hears is a bleeding man in irons shouting at a table. The singed guard snorts. The Foreman only waits for the echo to die, folds his hands on the beads, and when he speaks, his voice is as soft as before.

"Bite out your tongue. That would be a waste, and I do not like waste." He nods slightly at the shuttered window. "I do not need the road, Emberhide. My scouts counted them. Ninety-four small hands, and Ushk's ten spears, and a soft-hearted sergeant leading them to your aunt's door. They are two days from the Border Stones, and I have a rider who can be at the Stones in one." He lets that sit. "I would prefer not to send him. It is a messy business, and Harrowmere has herders with bows."

The beads click once. "So. A bargain, since you are a man who keeps his word. I know that of you. Gorrak told me, six years of his dull reports. You will swear to me, on your father's blood, as you swore to him, that you will serve Greyshaft as a hand until I judge the count of ninety-four made good. I will judge it fairly, and I do not hurry. In return I cut your sister's cords tonight, send her to Harrowmere with a safe-conduct and a mule, and call back my rider. The captain I leave to you." His eyes slide to Gorrak. "I am told he is dear to you now."

On the floor Tessa stirs, once, and a small sound comes out of her, half a breath, half a word. Gorrak has turned his face to the wall.

You have given the Tallies a year and a day and have eleven months of it still owing. You are at 1 hit point, in chains, and the Foreman is waiting with his pen already raised over a fresh line. What does Korvash say or do?

"Cut Tessa's cords now, call back your rider in front of me, and loose Gorrak, and I'll swear it on Dargan's blood. Her safe-conduct and a mule go first, and then my word."
